## 24. Should You Bootstrap Or Raise Funding For Your Startup? (pros and cons)
**Channel:** Clement Mihailescu | **Views:** 10K | **Date:** 2 years ago | **Duration:** 15:05 | **ID:** RSI30kV11fA
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=RSI30kV11fA

### Transcript:
what's up everybody has to go in in this video I want to talk about the pros and cons of bootstrapping a company versus those of fundraising for a company for those of you who don't know bootstrapping is when you build a company without any outside funding so either you are the only person you and your co-founders who put money into the business or you just don't put any money in the business and don't take any outside money and fundraising is obviously when you raise money from investors usually Venture capitalists if we're talking about tech companies now as many of you know I've been running my company algo expert for six years now and we have been fully bootstrapped from the very beginning we have never raised any money from outside investors and we put very little money into the business we only put about two thousand five hundred dollars each me and my co-founder Antoine and we've been profitable since basically the very beginning we've been able to sustain ourselves since the beginning that being said we have been approached by investors many many times times especially over the last three years investors asking us if we want fundraise we've considered it we've analyzed the pros and cons and we came to the conclusion that we wanted to stay bootstrapped and it has worked wonders for us I'm very very happy that we remained bootstrapped I'm very very happy that we never raised even a dollar from outside funding and in this video I'm going to share all these pros and cons by the way if you're a software engineer preparing for a technical interviews do check out my company I'll go expert go to algoexport.io and use a promo code clemclm for a discount on the platform so let's start with the five pros of bootstrapping your company Pro number one is that you do not give up any Equity this one might sound pretty obvious but it actually isn't when you think about the actual ramifications of giving up Equity because you see a lot of people assume that when you fundraise you are able to build bigger companies that can have a bigger X it's like a bigger IPO or a bigger you know acquisition meaning that they will make more money now this might be true that you can have a bigger exit and we'll talk about that when we talk about the pros of fundraising however does a bigger exit always lead to a bigger payout well if you've given up a lot of equity that may not be the case in fact it often is not the case let's look at two simple examples imagine you've launched a company that you have bootstrapped from the ground up and you get an awesome exit of 50 million dollars and let's assume that you've got two co-founders who own the entirety of the company and they've split the company 50 50. so an exit of 50 million dollars they're each gonna get 25 million dollars now to most people that is pretty darn good of a payout now let's assume that these same two Founders decide to build another company and this time they fundraise they raise a lot of money and they're able to grow that company into something much bigger and let's say they get an exit for 200 million dollars right a 4X bigger exit well let's look at the amount of equity dilution that might have gotten them to that 200 million dollar exit perhaps they went to a startup accelerator like y combinator so right off the get-go they gave away 7.5 of their company so they were left with 92.5 sent or something like 46.5 or 0.75 percent each of the company so already they've each lost like three and a half percent then they do a series a round or perhaps a seed round before a series a then they do a series a then a series B by the end of it it is very very likely for each co-founder to be left with 10 or less of their company and so ten percent on a 200 million dollar exit is going to leave you with how much 20 million dollars and so in this case the two co-founders made less money from a 200 million dollar exit that had a lot of fundraising and Equity being given up then in the case of the bootstrapped exit at 50 million dollars now of course I want to make it clear that my particular example here was one where the founders made less money there are countless examples where they may end up making more money perhaps they didn't give up that much Equity to where they were only left with 10 percent or perhaps they get a bigger exit of 300 million 400 million 900 million dollars who knows but the point is the example that I gave is not uncommon at all you really have to think about this when you start fundraising and this becomes especially important the more co-founders you have if suddenly you have three co-founders or four co-founders it is very easy to find yourself even with a billion dollar exit not making that much money or as much money as you would imagine you deserve from such an exit the second Pro of bootstrapping your company is that you do not give up any control in your company when you fundraise especially the more you fundraise the more you get bigger and bigger rounds of investment the more you give up control in your company typically because typically you start to have a board at your company and investors start to get board seats on that board and slowly but surely you start to lose control of your own company where you get less of a say in the decision-making process because perhaps you have to get board approval and sometimes you don't even get a say at all some of you might be familiar with the example of uber and Uber's founder Travis kalanick who was ousted from the company by its board by its investors back in like 2018 or 2019 and no matter what you think about Travis kalanick as a person whether or not he deserved it or whatever I'm pretty sure that if you were him you would have not wanted to be ousted from your own company that's what happens when you give up control and when you're bootstrapped you don't give up any control on a related note Pro number three is that you don't put any outside pressure on yourself when you are bootstrapped because you see once you start raising money from investors you have this outside pressure because you suddenly have this fiduciary duty to your investors you must give them a return on their investment you have to put their Capital to good use and so you can find yourself in positions where investors want you to do one thing with your company you want to do another thing and it's just a boatload of stress that you really wish you hadn't when you're bootstrapped you report to No One you don't have this outside pressure you can choose how to ride different markets you can choose the direction that you want to bring your company in you're not going to be pressured to sell you're not going to be pressured to raise more money you're not going to be pressured to hire or fire certain people you get to decide Pro number four by being bootstrapped you force yourself to have to do more with less because naturally you have fewer resources when you are bootstrapped primarily Financial Resources but by extension other resources like fewer employees because you don't have the same amount of money to employ those employees fewer software services that you can use to empower your employees or your company you might not be able to get into the biggest office or be able to give the biggest perch because you don't have the money to do these things but that doesn't necessarily put you at a disadvantage it just teaches you to do more with less you start to realize that maybe two really really good employees is just as good as 5 or 10 or 20 modest employees decent employees you become much more disciplined with cash you really think of where you are allocating your cash because you don't have a two billion dollar check that you can rely on obviously I'm exaggerating a little bit with two billion dollars but you get the idea and finally Pro number five is that you get to focus all of your time and energy and resources on actually building your company and not on fundraising as a lot of people who have fundraised a lot will tell you fundraising takes a lot of time a lot of effort it's apparently not easy especially if you're kind of a nobody or this is your first company or if your company doesn't really have a lot of you know promising like revenue or promising traction or if it's going through a hard time it takes a ton of time and effort raising funds and that detracts you from your main mission which is to build a company I hear from a lot of VC backed CEOs that something like half their time if not more is spent on getting ready to raise the next round and it's this Perpetual never-ending cycle and that's just like a totally different job and it it just you know a lot of people might not like that so that's just something to think about when you're making the decision of whether or not to stay bootstrapped and like I said for me these five Pros have been absolutely wonderful and I'm so happy that we have remained bootstrapped on algo expert that being said I realize that fundraising isn't only negative it's not only bad there are positives to fundraising so let's dive into them the first pro is that you can presumably grow much faster than if you stay bootstrapped why because the more you have money like if suddenly I give you 20 million dollars to build whatever company website app you can start hiring a lot of people to help you build that you can start paying a lot of money for advertising or for business operations business Partnerships and that can presumably help you grow now again here there are a lot of caveats like I said before I don't actually think that blindly hiring more people especially more Engineers is necessarily going to make you grow faster but nonetheless I do think that Common Sense dictates that the more money you have the more you can likely do and that is one of the pros of raising funds the second Pro is that you can build companies of truly massive scale the truth is there are certain companies that can never be built without raising money there are companies where the very business model of those companies is such that it requires a lot of capital to get the company up and running and the company will not be profitable for a long time and so you need to keep pouring Capital into the company and oftentimes these are giant companies these are some of the biggest companies that you see around you and the grand majority of them are admittedly not bootstrapped because it's just not possible so if you truly want to build a giant company or a company whose business model prevents it from being profitable immediately then fundraising is going to be very useful and perhaps even necessary the third Pro is that by fundraising you can get a lot of publicity this is especially true when you raise a lot of money so if you start raising you know a huge series a or an even bigger series b a 200 million dollar series C or something like that you're gonna get a lot of publicity at least in mainstream Tech media so things like you know Tech crunch or even mainstream media like you might be picked up on the cnns and foxes and bloombergs of the world now this is also kind of debatable because in this day and age I firmly believe that you should be in charge of your own publicity you should try to build an online brand on YouTube Twitter company grow now the fourth Pro is that you can actually get a lot of help from some of your investors there are a lot of really talented really helpful investors out there and by getting money from them not only can you get these investors as sort of mentors as advisors you can even call them up on the phone in the middle of the night and ask them for help on certain things that they've likely experienced before with other companies especially big companies but also you can tap into their networks oftentimes you can hire a lot of people through these Venture capitalists sort of hiring ## 28. The Best Algorithm Ever - The Ostrich Algorithm
**Channel:** Clement Mihailescu | **Views:** 15K | **Date:** 3 years ago | **Duration:** 5:15 | **ID:** uHjbdvDs6Jk

what's up everybody how's it going in this video I want to talk about a very fun but obscure algorithm or Concept in programming that I happen to really really love and agree with which is why I'm gonna coin it the best algorithm ever and this is none other than the ostrich algorithm named after ostriches the animal those big birds that kind of not really have wings but sort of no and what the ostrich algorithm or concept States is that if you ever encounter in programming a situation where you have some code and this code has a bug in it but that bug is very very unlikely to ever occur though it can occur and fixing that bug or preventing the bug from you know popping up is a very time consuming or costly then you should just ignore it that is what the ostrich algorithm says says and the reason it's named after ostriches is because Legend has it that ostriches when they are in danger bury their head in the sand so as to just ignore the imminent danger they don't run away from it and they don't try to face it they just bury their head in the sand and here it's the same thing instead of fixing the bug you just say you know you know what it's probably unlikely to happen so I'm not gonna spend the time to fix it and the reason that I really love the ostrich algorithm and really agree with it is because I am someone who is very pragmatic very practical I've said this in previous videos on this channel I have always believed that you should put the least amount of effort or spend the least amount of resources to accomplish your goals in a way that is satisfactory so sometimes that does mean going the extra mile putting the extra effort but oftentimes that means not doing that extra sort of 10 or even 80 of the work because oftentimes with 20 of the work you can get eighty percent of the outcome and so here if you kind of extrapolate this to tied back to the ostrich algorithm when you have a potential bug that is extremely unlikely to happen the pragmatic in me just finds it really really really not worth it to spend the time or research sources fixing that bug and this is something that we have done many times on algo expert in my company by the way if you're a software engineer preparing for technical interviews you check out algo expert go to algoexport.io and use promo code Clemson for discount on the platform but there have been many times where we had this small likelihood that a bug would occur we knew about that bug but fixing it would either take a lot of time or it would make the code very messy very complicated and so we chose to ignore it now of course this is an art this is a balancing act you cannot do this all the time I'm not advocating that you leave bugs everywhere in your code base no a lot of bugs that can be fixed in a medium let's say amount of time are worth fixing but for certain edge cases and you kind of know them when you encounter them it is worth just applying the ostrich algorithm and what's interesting is that I think the ostrich algorithm doesn't only have to do with bugs sometimes you can apply the ostrich algorithm to other things for example again on algo expert there have been a couple times where we had to make a change to an API for example for a new feature and we could either do it the right way or the ostrich way the right way would involve maybe recreating a brand new API or really making drastic changes to the existing API to make it more you know semantically accurate to make it more consumable by Third parties things like that or we could just ostrich it up bury your head in the sand and say okay nobody's realistically ever gonna use this API other than the internal algo expert team and so therefore spending the time to recreate this API or make these drastic changes that are going to break a ton of other things in our code base that we're gonna then have to go fix and blah blah blah and potentially introduce other bugs instead we're just gonna slap an extra field in the API the existing API in a place that's not very good not very correct it certainly wouldn't pass Google public API reviews but let's ostrich it up it's worth it and we've done that multiple times it
